Mostly it’s the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”

I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happening all over the place. It’s really tied to that straight razor.

Like, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it. But when that razor is sitting open at my station, it just feels different. My mind goes straight to, “I’m about to cut myself badly,” or worse, “I’m going to hurt somebody.”

Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. But at work, I’ve gotten bad with it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to not have to use one. I’ll try to switch the client to someone else, or I’ll say the blade isn’t around, or I’ll just shove it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there open where I can see it. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Trying to keep my hand steady and act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of using the straight razor caused you any problems at work, like avoiding certain tasks or clients, or do you feel it’s starting to affect your job performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems now. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.

But it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.

The main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.

It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.

But at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. And if I can’t get out of it, I just kind of force myself through it, but I’m shaking the whole time. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.

And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.

It’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.

And then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.

It started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.

But yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.

Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s weird because I can look at other sharp stuff in the shop and not totally lose it, but that razor… I don’t know. Even the way it folds open kind of gets in my head. I’ll start thinking about it instead of the haircut, and then I’m off worrying about whether my hand is steady enough. So yeah, it’s mostly that situation.\n\nDoctor: When you’re faced with using the straight razor, does your anxiety build up quickly, like a panic attack, or does it feel more like a general, ongoing worry?\n\nPatient: It’s quick. It’s like my body wants to back up before I’ve even had time to think about it.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to act normal, you know, because I’m at work and there are mirrors everywhere, people can see your face from every angle—sorry, that’s probably not the point. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mostly deal with straight razors at work, do you ever feel anxious or distressed just thinking about them when you're not at the shop, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.\n\nIt’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.\n\nBut yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself avoiding certain thoughts, places, or activities related to straight razors, even outside of work, because they make you anxious?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s more like I try not to let my brain go there. Like if I start thinking about the razor, I’ll distract myself, put on something, scroll my phone, whatever.

But the big avoidance is definitely at the shop. That’s kind of why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which is embarrassing, because it’s part of the job.

And if I can’t get out of it, I still do it, but I’m shaking through the shave. Trying to act normal, talking to the client about their weekend or football or whatever, but inside I’m just waiting for it to be over.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of ridiculous for me. I’m a barber, and anytime it comes to using the straight razor, I’ll do almost anything to get out of it. Like, I’ll switch clients with someone, or say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer and pretend it’s not there.\n\nIt sounds stupid saying it out loud—my coworker actually jokes that I’m “organizing” when I’m really just hiding stuff—but if I can’t avoid it and I have to do the shave, my hands are shaking the whole time. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or panicking in other situations, or is it mostly just when you have to use the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s weird because I can look at other sharp stuff in the shop and not totally lose it, but that razor… I don’t know. Even the way it folds open kind of gets in my head. I’ll start thinking about it instead of the haircut, and then I’m off worrying about whether my hand is steady enough. So yeah, it’s mostly that situation.\n\nDoctor: When you’re faced with using the straight razor, does your anxiety build up quickly, like a panic attack, or does it feel more like a general, ongoing worry?\n\nPatient: It’s quick. Like, really quick. The second I hear someone ask for a razor shave, or if I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens right away and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back up before I’ve even had time to think about it.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to act normal, you know, because I’m at work and there are mirrors everywhere, people can see your face from every angle—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yeah, it hits immediately, not like some slow worry that builds all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in other situations outside of work, or is this kind of sudden anxiety only tied to the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happening all over the place. It’s really tied to that straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it. But when that razor is sitting open at my station, it just feels different. My mind goes straight to, “I’m about to cut myself badly,” or worse, “I’m going to hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s annoying because in my head I know that’s not what’s happening, but in the moment it feels really real. Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. But at work, I’ve gotten bad with it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to not have to use one. I’ll try to switch the client to someone else, or I’ll say the blade isn’t around, or I’ll just shove it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there open where I can see it. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Trying to keep my hand steady and act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of using the straight razor caused you any problems at work, like avoiding certain tasks or clients, or do you feel it’s starting to affect your job performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems now. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.\n\nBut it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.\n\nThe main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided situations outside of work because you might come into contact with a straight razor, or is this something that only really affects you while you're at work?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.\n\nBut at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to not use one—switch clients, say the blade’s missing, tuck it away in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… not so much personal grooming, because I don’t really use one on myself. I’ve got a trimmer at home, and honestly those little bathroom drawers get messy anyway—chargers, combs, old razors, all that stuff.

But the real thing is at work. That’s why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor. I’ll try to switch clients, or I’ll be like, “Oh, I can’t find the blade,” or I’ll just put it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there staring at me. It sounds stupid saying it out loud.

And if I can’t get out of it, I do it, but I’m shaking through the whole shave, just trying to keep the conversation normal like nothing’s happening.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of ridiculous for me. I’m a barber, and anytime it comes to using the straight razor, I’ll do almost anything to get out of it. Like, I’ll switch clients with someone, or say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer and pretend it’s not there.\n\nIt sounds stupid saying it out loud—my coworker actually jokes that I’m “organizing” when I’m really just hiding stuff—but if I can’t avoid it and I have to do the shave, my hands are shaking the whole time. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or panicking in other situations, or is it mostly just when you have to use the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s weird because I can look at other sharp stuff in the shop and not totally lose it, but that razor… I don’t know. Even the way it folds open kind of gets in my head. I’ll start thinking about it instead of the haircut, and then I’m off worrying about whether my hand is steady enough. So yeah, it’s mostly that situation.\n\nDoctor: When you’re faced with using the straight razor, does your anxiety build up quickly, like a panic attack, or does it feel more like a general, ongoing worry?\n\nPatient: It’s quick. Like, really quick. The second I hear someone ask for a razor shave, or if I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens right away and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back up before I’ve even had time to think about it.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to act normal, you know, because I’m at work and there are mirrors everywhere, people can see your face from every angle—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yeah, it hits immediately, not like some slow worry that builds all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in other situations outside of work, or is this kind of sudden anxiety only tied to the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happening all over the place. It’s really tied to that straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it. But when that razor is sitting open at my station, it just feels different. My mind goes straight to, “I’m about to cut myself badly,” or worse, “I’m going to hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s annoying because in my head I know that’s not what’s happening, but in the moment it feels really real. Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. But at work, I’ve gotten bad with it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to not have to use one. I’ll try to switch the client to someone else, or I’ll say the blade isn’t around, or I’ll just shove it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there open where I can see it. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Trying to keep my hand steady and act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of using the straight razor caused you any problems at work, like avoiding certain tasks or clients, or do you feel it’s starting to affect your job performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems now. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.\n\nBut it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.\n\nThe main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided situations outside of work because you might come into contact with a straight razor, or is this something that only really affects you while you're at work?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.\n\nBut at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to not use one—switch clients, say the blade’s missing, tuck it away in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which sounds crazy when I say it, because I used to be proud of doing clean razor work.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just kind of force myself through it, but I’m shaking the whole time. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mostly deal with straight razors at work, do you ever feel anxious or distressed just thinking about them when you're not at the shop, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.\n\nIt’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.\n\nBut it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.\n\nThe main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided situations outside of work because you might come into contact with a straight razor, or is this something that only really affects you while you're at work?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.\n\nBut at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to not use one—switch clients, say the blade’s missing, tuck it away in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which sounds crazy when I say it, because I used to be proud of doing clean razor work.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just kind of force myself through it, but I’m shaking the whole time. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mostly deal with straight razors at work, do you ever feel anxious or distressed just thinking about them when you're not at the shop, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.\n\nIt’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.\n\nBut yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain thoughts, places, or activities related to straight razors, even outside of work, because they make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s more like I try not to let my brain go there. Like if I start thinking about the razor, I’ll distract myself, put on something, scroll my phone, whatever.\n\nBut the big avoidance is definitely at the shop. That’s kind of why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which is embarrassing, because it’s part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I still do it, but I’m shaking through the shave. Trying to act normal, talking to the client about their weekend or football or whatever, but inside I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided using a straight razor for personal grooming or when seeing one outside of work, like in a store or someone else’s home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much personal grooming, because I don’t really use one on myself. I’ve got a trimmer at home, and honestly those little bathroom drawers get messy anyway—chargers, combs, old razors, all that stuff.\n\nBut the real thing is at work. That’s why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor. I’ll try to switch clients, or I’ll be like, “Oh, I can’t find the blade,” or I’ll just put it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there staring at me. It sounds stupid saying it out loud.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I do it, but I’m shaking through the whole shave, just trying to keep the conversation normal like nothing’s happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s weird because I can look at other sharp stuff in the shop and not totally lose it, but that razor… I don’t know. Even the way it folds open kind of gets in my head. I’ll start thinking about it instead of the haircut, and then I’m off worrying about whether my hand is steady enough. So yeah, it’s mostly that situation.\n\nDoctor: When you’re faced with using the straight razor, does your anxiety build up quickly, like a panic attack, or does it feel more like a general, ongoing worry?\n\nPatient: It’s quick. Like, really quick. The second I hear someone ask for a razor shave, or if I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens right away and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back up before I’ve even had time to think about it.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to act normal, you know, because I’m at work and there are mirrors everywhere, people can see your face from every angle—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yeah, it hits immediately, not like some slow worry that builds all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in other situations outside of work, or is this kind of sudden anxiety only tied to the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happening all over the place. It’s really tied to that straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it. But when that razor is sitting open at my station, it just feels different. My mind goes straight to, “I’m about to cut myself badly,” or worse, “I’m going to hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s annoying because in my head I know that’s not what’s happening, but in the moment it feels really real. Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. But at work, I’ve gotten bad with it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to not have to use one. I’ll try to switch the client to someone else, or I’ll say the blade isn’t around, or I’ll just shove it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there open where I can see it. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Trying to keep my hand steady and act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of using the straight razor caused you any problems at work, like avoiding certain tasks or clients, or do you feel it’s starting to affect your job performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems now. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.\n\nBut it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.\n\nThe main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided situations outside of work because you might come into contact with a straight razor, or is this something that only really affects you while you're at work?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.\n\nBut at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to not use one—switch clients, say the blade’s missing, tuck it away in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which sounds crazy when I say it, because I used to be proud of doing clean razor work.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just kind of force myself through it, but I’m shaking the whole time. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mostly deal with straight razors at work, do you ever feel anxious or distressed just thinking about them when you're not at the shop, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.\n\nIt’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.\n\nBut yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain thoughts, places, or activities related to straight razors, even outside of work, because they make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s more like I try not to let my brain go there. Like if I start thinking about the razor, I’ll distract myself, put on something, scroll my phone, whatever.\n\nBut the big avoidance is definitely at the shop. That’s kind of why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which is embarrassing, because it’s part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I still do it, but I’m shaking through the shave. Trying to act normal, talking to the client about their weekend or football or whatever, but inside I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided using a straight razor for personal grooming or when seeing one outside of work, like in a store or someone else’s home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much personal grooming, because I don’t really use one on myself. I’ve got a trimmer at home, and honestly those little bathroom drawers get messy anyway—chargers, combs, old razors, all that stuff.\n\nBut the real thing is at work. That’s why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor. I’ll try to switch clients, or I’ll be like, “Oh, I can’t find the blade,” or I’ll just put it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there staring at me. It sounds stupid saying it out loud.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I do it, but I’m shaking through the whole shave, just trying to keep the conversation normal like nothing’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided something—like a TV show, a movie, or even a conversation—because it reminded you of using the straight razor, even if you weren't actually at work?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, not like I’m walking out of movies or anything, but if something reminds me of it, my brain goes right back to the shop.\n\nHonestly, that’s the main place it happens. I’m here because I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to avoid using the straight razor at work. I’ll switch clients if I can, or say the blade’s missing, or just stick it in the drawer so I don’t have to see it sitting there.\n\nIt’s weird, because I can talk to people all day about random stuff—sports, their kids, whatever—and act normal, but if I can’t get out of doing the shave, I’m shaking through the whole thing, just trying to get it over with.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt similar anxiety or the urge to avoid something when you see objects that look like a straight razor, even if they aren’t one—like a letter opener, a pocket knife, or something else that’s sharp and metallic?\n\nPatient: I mean… sharp metal stuff can catch my eye sometimes, yeah, but it’s really the straight razor at the shop that does it. Like a pocket knife or whatever, I might notice it and think, “Ugh,” but then I’m kind of back to whatever I was doing.\n\nAt work, though, it’s different. That’s why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say the blade’s missing, or just hide it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. And if I can’t get out of it, I still do the shave, but I’m shaking through the whole thing and just trying to act normal until it’s over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F40.290", "F40.10"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say the blade’s missing, or just hide it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. And if I can’t get out of it, I still do the shave, but I’m shaking through the whole thing and just trying to act normal until it’s over.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt anxious or avoided using other sharp tools or objects—like a kitchen knife, a box cutter, or a scalpel—even outside of work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.290, F40.10\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of ridiculous for me. I’m a barber, and anytime it comes to using the straight razor, I’ll do almost anything to get out of it. Like, I’ll switch clients with someone, or say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer and pretend it’s not there.\n\nIt sounds stupid saying it out loud—my coworker actually jokes that I’m “organizing” when I’m really just hiding stuff—but if I can’t avoid it and I have to do the shave, my hands are shaking the whole time. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or panicking in other situations, or is it mostly just when you have to use the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors too, no big deal, and I know the razor is just a tool—I trained with it, I’ve used it before—but when it’s sitting there open at my station, my brain just goes, like, “You’re about to cut yourself badly,” or “You’re gonna hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s weird because I can look at other sharp stuff in the shop and not totally lose it, but that razor… I don’t know. Even the way it folds open kind of gets in my head. I’ll start thinking about it instead of the haircut, and then I’m off worrying about whether my hand is steady enough. So yeah, it’s mostly that situation.\n\nDoctor: When you’re faced with using the straight razor, does your anxiety build up quickly, like a panic attack, or does it feel more like a general, ongoing worry?\n\nPatient: It’s quick. Like, really quick. The second I hear someone ask for a razor shave, or if I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens right away and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back up before I’ve even had time to think about it.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to act normal, you know, because I’m at work and there are mirrors everywhere, people can see your face from every angle—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yeah, it hits immediately, not like some slow worry that builds all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in other situations outside of work, or is this kind of sudden anxiety only tied to the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happening all over the place. It’s really tied to that straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it. But when that razor is sitting open at my station, it just feels different. My mind goes straight to, “I’m about to cut myself badly,” or worse, “I’m going to hurt somebody.”\n\nIt’s annoying because in my head I know that’s not what’s happening, but in the moment it feels really real. Like the razor is the whole problem, not just work in general.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever find yourself avoiding anything related to straight razors, or thinking about them in a distressing way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much outside work, because I don’t really have straight razors around at home or anything. But at work, I’ve gotten bad with it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to not have to use one. I’ll try to switch the client to someone else, or I’ll say the blade isn’t around, or I’ll just shove it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there open where I can see it. Which is ridiculous, because I’m a barber, you know? Like, this is part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just… I’m shaking through the whole shave. Trying to keep my hand steady and act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of using the straight razor caused you any problems at work, like avoiding certain tasks or clients, or do you feel it’s starting to affect your job performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems now. I’ve lost tips because I’ll kind of dodge the razor shave or pass it off to another barber, and some regulars have complained about it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because, like, people come to a barber expecting you can do the whole service. And the shop gets this vibe sometimes where everyone’s joking around, music’s on, people are talking sports or whatever, and I’m over here trying to figure out how to not pick up one tool. It makes me feel stupid.\n\nI even backed out of a barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I made it sound like I had a scheduling issue, but really it was the razor part. So yeah, it’s not just in my head anymore—it’s messing with work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this fear of straight razors affect any other parts of your life, like your relationships, hobbies, or how you act in public?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I don’t know, not a ton, honestly. I mean, it’s mostly wrapped up in the shop because that’s where the straight razors are.\n\nBut it does kind of spill over in the sense that I’m stressed about work more than I used to be. Like, I’ll be hanging out with friends and start thinking about whether a regular is going to ask for a razor shave the next day. And then I’m not really present.\n\nThe main damage is still at work, though. I’ve lost tips, had regular clients complain because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber. And I even backed out of this barber battle I was supposed to do because they required razor detailing. I told people it was a scheduling thing, but it wasn’t. It was the razor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided situations outside of work because you might come into contact with a straight razor, or is this something that only really affects you while you're at work?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly work. Like, I’m not walking around the grocery store worried someone’s gonna pull out a straight razor or anything.\n\nBut at the shop, yeah, that’s where it gets bad. I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to not use one—switch clients, say the blade’s missing, tuck it away in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which sounds crazy when I say it, because I used to be proud of doing clean razor work.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I just kind of force myself through it, but I’m shaking the whole time. Like trying to act normal while my hand is not cooperating. So outside work, not really… but because work is such a big part of my life, it feels like it follows me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mostly deal with straight razors at work, do you ever feel anxious or distressed just thinking about them when you're not at the shop, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. Like if I’m at home and I know I’ve got a client the next day who usually wants a razor finish, it’ll pop into my head.\n\nIt’s weird because I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, you know? I can use clippers all day, scissors, guards, all that stuff without even thinking. But when I picture that straight razor sitting open at my station, it feels different. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.\n\nBut yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain thoughts, places, or activities related to straight razors, even outside of work, because they make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s more like I try not to let my brain go there. Like if I start thinking about the razor, I’ll distract myself, put on something, scroll my phone, whatever.\n\nBut the big avoidance is definitely at the shop. That’s kind of why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it. Which is embarrassing, because it’s part of the job.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it, I still do it, but I’m shaking through the shave. Trying to act normal, talking to the client about their weekend or football or whatever, but inside I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or avoided using a straight razor for personal grooming or when seeing one outside of work, like in a store or someone else’s home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much personal grooming, because I don’t really use one on myself. I’ve got a trimmer at home, and honestly those little bathroom drawers get messy anyway—chargers, combs, old razors, all that stuff.\n\nBut the real thing is at work. That’s why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor. I’ll try to switch clients, or I’ll be like, “Oh, I can’t find the blade,” or I’ll just put it in the drawer so it’s not sitting there staring at me. It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself really bad.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of runs with it. I’ll be trying to watch a game with friends or something, and I’m thinking about this tiny blade instead of the score, which is ridiculous. But yeah, even when I’m not at the shop, just thinking about it can make me feel messed up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this way about using the straight razor—has this fear been building up over months, or is it something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: It’s been… almost a year now, I guess. Like around 11 months.\n\nIt started after me and the shop owner kept getting into it about that straight-razor shave package he wanted us pushing. At first I thought I was just irritated about the pressure, you know, like everybody’s always trying to upsell something now—hair products, beard oils, all that stuff—and customers can tell when it’s forced.\n\nBut yeah, somewhere around then it stopped being just annoying and turned into this whole thing with the razor. So… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain thoughts, places, or activities related to straight razors, even outside of work, because they make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s more like I try not to let my brain go there. Like if I start thinking about the razor, I’ll distract myself, put on something, scroll my phone, whatever.\n\nBut the big avoidance is definitely at the shop. That’s kind of why I’m here. I’ve gotten to where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor—switch clients with someone, say I can’t find the blade, or just shove it in the drawer so I don’t have to look at it.
